Technical extraction; compare with original PDF page 1.
Optimal Inference of the Mean Outcome under Optimal Treatment
Regime
Shuoxun Xu∗
Xinzhou Guo†
September 15, 2026
Abstract
When an optimal treatment regime (OTR) is considered, we need to evaluate the OTR in
a valid and efficient way. The classical inference applied to the mean outcome under OTR,
assuming the OTR is the same as the estimated OTR, might be biased when the regularity
assumption that OTR is unique is violated. Although several methods have been proposed to
allow nonregularity in such inference, its optimality is unclear due to challenges in deriving semi-
parametric efficiency bounds under potential nonregularity. In this paper, we address the bias
issue via adaptive smoothing over the estimated OTR and develop a valid inference procedure
on the mean outcome under OTR regardless of whether regularity is satisfied. We establish the
optimality of the proposed method by deriving a lower bound of the asymptotic variance for the
robust asymptotically linear unbiased estimator to the mean outcome under OTR and showing
that our proposed estimator achieves the variance lower bound. The considered estimator class
is general and the derived variance lower bound paves a novel way to establish efficiency opti-
mality theories for OTR in a more general scenario allowing nonregularity. The merit of the
proposed method is demonstrated by re-analyzing the ACTG 175 trial.
Keywords: Adaptive smoothing, Bias correction, Nonregularity, Robust asymptotically linear
unbiased estimators
1
Introduction
The optimal treatment regime (OTR) refers to a personalized treatment assignment strategy de-
signed to maximize the average clinical benefit across a population (Li et al., 2023). In the era of
precision medicine, the study of OTR plays an increasingly important role as targeted therapies of-
ten exhibit treatment effect heterogeneity and the “one-size-fits-all” strategy might be suboptimal;
∗Department of Mathematics, The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology, Clear Water Bay, Kowloon,
Hong Kong, China; Division of Biostatistics, School of Public Health, University of California, Berkeley, California
94720-7360, USA.
†Department of Mathematics, The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology, Clear Water Bay, Kowloon,
Hong Kong, China. Corresponding author: xinzhoug@ust.hk.
1
arXiv:2509.09773v3  [stat.ME]  15 Sep 2026


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 2 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 2.
see the discussion in Malone et al. (2014). One condition where OTR has been intensively studied
is the human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection (Farahmandpour et al., 2023). For example,
Fan et al. (2017) found that ZDV+ddl is more favorable than ZDV+zal for HIV-infected patients
aged over 37.5 and vice versa, and age is a crucial factor in the clinical practice of prescribing
drugs for HIV-infected patients (Cardoso et al., 2013). OTR has also been widely studied in other
conditions, such as breast cancer (Sparano et al., 2018), type II diabetes (Gæde et al., 2008) and
hypertension (The SPRINT Research Group, 2015). A global market of 614.22 Billion USD is
projected for precision medicine in 2024 (Research, 2024).
Given the practical importance of OTR, various methodologies have been developed to esti-
mate the OTR over the past decades, which typically fall into two categories, direct and indirect
approaches (Chakraborty et al., 2014). The direct approach first specifies a class of treatment
regimes (TR) and estimates the corresponding value function, and then the OTR is selected as the
TR maximizing the value function (Orellana et al., 2010a,b). The indirect approach first models
the stage-specific conditional mean outcomes or contrasts, and then the OTR is found via maxi-
mization of these estimated conditional means or contrasts (Moodie et al., 2012; Zhao et al., 2012).
By 2026, there have been 1,540 publications about “optimal treatment regime” in google scholar
(Google, 2026).
Besides the estimation of OTR, how to conduct valid and efficient statistical inference on the
mean outcome under OTR is a critical question in drug development and regulatory decision-making
(Chakraborty and Murphy, 2014). This is because the mean outcome under OTR measures how
good an OTR is and helps decide whether the OTR should be further pursued and implemented
(Wang et al., 2012). For example, Lavori and Dawson (2004) evaluated and compared the mean
outcomes of two treatment regimes for schizophrenic prodrome, and the better one is later suggested
in clinical practices. To infer the mean outcome under OTR, Zhang et al. (2012) and Zhang et al.
(2013a) proposed plug-in methods, and van der Laan and Luedtke (2014) and Luedtke and van der
Laan (2016) established semiparametric optimality. However, the validity and the optimality of
existing methods typically rely on the regularity assumption that the OTR is unique.
In practice, the regularity assumption might be violated much more frequently than we expect.
In particular, nonregularity arises when the treatments have no individualized clinical differences in
a non-negligible subgroup. For example, Sax et al. (2009) found that among a subgroup of patients
2


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 3 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 3.
with screening HIV-1 RNA levels equal to or above 100,000 copies per milliliter, the compared
two treatments, Abacavir-Lamivudine and Tenofovir-Emtricitabine, have no clinical differences
w.r.t. the change from the baseline CD4 cell count at week 48. Such nonregularity can break
the validity and optimality of the classical approach for inference on the mean outcome under
OTR. Specifically, under nonregularity, the classical approach, which simply plugs in the estimated
OTR by assuming it is the same as the OTR, often suffers from bias issues because the estimated
OTR is inconsistent when the OTR is not uniquely defined as discussed in Shi et al. (2020).
Moreover, nonregularity hinders the establishment of efficiency optimality of the estimators because
the classical semiparametric efficiency bound is established for regular estimators which cannot be
appropriately defined or naturally extended due to the lack of path-wise differentiability under
nonregularity (van der Laan and Rose, 2011; Luedtke and van der Laan, 2016).
Some attempts have been made to address the bias issue and improve efficiency in inference
on the mean outcome under OTR when nonregularity is allowed. Wager and Athey (2018) and
Wang et al. (2018) considered simple sample split methods, which clearly suffer from efficiency loss.
Chakraborty et al. (2010, 2014) proposed to use m-out-of-n bootstrap to correctly approximate the
distribution of the plug-in estimator, but the length of the proposed confidence interval (CI) shrinks
at the rate of m−1/2. Luedtke and van der Laan (2016) proposed a one-step online estimator to
achieve √n-consistency but due to the sequential way of constructing the estimator, this approach
is sensitive to how the data are processed and the length of the proposed CI is longer than that
of the oracle estimator which assumes we know the true OTR. Based on the idea of subbagging
and refitted cross-validation, a current state-of-the-art approach was proposed by Shi et al. (2020)
which can achieve better efficiency than that of the oracle estimator via intensive computation.
Although existing works keep improving the efficiency of inference on the mean outcome under
OTR, the optimality theory is still lacking under potential nonregularity.
It remains unknown
where the pursuit of efficiency improvement ends and how efficiency-optimal inference on the mean
outcome under OTR can be achieved when nonregularity is allowed.
To bridge the gap, we propose an adaptive smoothing approach to infer the mean outcome under
OTR. We show that with appropriate smoothing to the estimated decision function of OTR, we can
address the bias issue induced by potential nonregularity without intensive computation and deliver
valid and efficient inference on the mean outcome under OTR regardless of whether it is regular
3


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 4 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 4.
or not. To establish the efficiency optimality of the proposed method, we define a class of robust
asymptotically linear unbiased estimators, derive its lower asymptotic variance bound and show that
the proposed method achieves the bound under both homoscedasticity and heteroscedasticity. The
considered class is free of path-wise differentiability conditions and includes the efficient regular, the
current state-of-the-art and many other valid estimators under both regularity and nonregularity,
and the derived variance lower bound provides the foundation for efficiency optimality theory for
OTR in a more general scenario allowing nonregularity where classical semiparametric theories fail.
Smoothing approaches have been considered in several related scenarios. Levis et al. (2025)
employs a global smoothing strategy via the log-sum-exp approximation for inference on covariate-
adjusted Balke-Pearl bounds, but directly applying their global smoothing to inference on the mean
outcome of OTR would introduce approximation error everywhere and might not fully resolve
bias.
Chen et al. (2023) proposes a softmax-based smoothing to deliver valid inference on the
optimal mean outcome when nonregularity is allowed, but relies on parametric structural models
and does not establish efficiency optimality. Whitehouse et al. (2025) extends Chen et al. (2023)
to a model-free setting and to more general maximum-type nonregular values than the optimal
mean outcome, yet efficiency optimality remains unresolved. Wu and Wang (2021) applies kernel
smoothing to infer the index parameter indexing the optimal regime within a parametric class;
however, their smoothing mainly targets the decision rule rather than the value inference and is
restricted to the regular case. Moreover, a common limitation across these approaches is that they
typically use a single tuning parameter controlling smoothness and assign symmetric weights at the
decision boundary, which might ensure validity but might not help them or their simple variants
achieve efficiency optimality. By contrast, our proposed method introduces an additional degree of
freedom that adapts the boundary weight to optimize efficiency without impacting validity, while a
separate smoothing parameter guarantees inference validity. This two-parameter smoothing scheme
substantially distinguishes our approach from existing smoothing methods and is precisely what
enables us to establish and achieve efficiency optimality under nonregularity, a long-standing open
problem in this area noted by van der Laan and Rose (2011) and Whitehouse et al. (2025).
In summary, the contributions of the paper lie in both methodology and theory.
Method-
ologically, we provide a valid and efficient way to assess the OTR when nonregularity is allowed,
outperforming existing methods in terms of efficiency. Theoretically, we establish the efficiency op-
4


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 5 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 5.
timality of the proposed method within a broad class of estimators allowing nonregularity, paving a
novel way to establish optimality theories in nonregular inference where path-wise differentiability
is lacking and classical semiparametric theories are not applicable.
The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we state the problem setting, il-
lustrate the bias under nonregularity, propose the adaptive smoothing method and investigate the
theoretical validity of the proposed method. In Section 3, we discuss the challenges of establishing
efficiency optimality under nonregularity, introduce the class of robust asymptotically linear unbi-
ased estimators and show that the proposed method is optimal within the class. In Section 4, we
compare the proposed method with the competing methods via simulation studies. In Section 5,
we re-analyze the ACTG 175 trial with the proposed method and show how much efficiency can be
gained with the proposed method in practice. A discussion is provided in Section 6.
2
Methodology
In this section, we start with mathematical formulations of the problem we consider and the bias is-
sue under nonregularity. Then, we introduce the proposed method based on an adaptive smoothing
on the estimated decision function, and investigate its theoretical validity and practical implemen-
tation in the single time point case of optimal treatment regimes. Extensions to multiple time point
cases; i.e., dynamic treatment regimes, can be found in Section B of the supplementary material.
2.1
Problem Setting
Consider an observational study of n patients with observations On = {(Xi, Ai, Yi), i = 1, . . . , n},
n i.i.d. copies of O = (X, A, Y ), where X ∈X denotes a patient’s baseline covariates, A ∈{0, 1}
denotes the treatment a patient receives and Y denotes a patient’s clinical outcome. Let Y (0) and
Y (1) be the potential outcomes under treatment 0 and 1 respectively and π(a, x) := P(A = a|X =
x) be the propensity score. Throughout the paper, we assume E[Y 2] < ∞and make the following
classical identifiability assumptions in observational studies (Ding, 2024).
Assumption 1 (Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption) Y = (1 −A) Y (0) + AY (1).
Assumption 2 (No Unmeasured Confounders) (Y (0), Y (1)) ⊥A | X.
5


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 6 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 6.
Assumption 3 (Overlap) ∃c ∈(0, 1/2) s.t. c < π(a, x) < 1 −c.
A treatment regime d(·) is a deterministic function that maps X to {0, 1}. Given a treatment
regime d, the potential outcome of a subject is Y (d) = Y (0) {1 −d (X)} + Y (1)d (X) and the mean
outcome of a population is V (d) = E {Y (d)}. An optimal treatment regime (OTR) dopt is defined
as a regime maximizing the mean outcome; i.e, dopt ∈arg maxd:X→{0,1} V (d). In this paper, we aim
to construct valid and efficiency-optimal confidence intervals (CIs) for the mean outcome under
OTR; i.e.,
V0 = V (dopt) = max
d
V (d),
(1)
as statistical inference on the mean outcome under OTR is crucial for validating the efficacy of
OTR, guiding clinical decisions and informing healthcare policies (Lavori and Dawson, 2004).
Note that the OTR, dopt, may not be unique. Denote the conditional mean functions of outcome
by µ(a, x) ≡E (Y | A = a, X = x) and the contrast function by τ(x) ≡µ(1, x) −µ(0, x). When
P(τ(X) = 0) > 0; i.e., the treatments have no differences in a non-negligible subgroup, the set of
OTR, Dopt = {dopt : V
 dopt
= maxd V (d)}, is not a singleton because for the subjects in {X :
τ(X) = 0}, taking either treatment or control would not change the mean outcome. This commonly
encountered scenario is referred to as nonregularity, which classical inference often excludes to avoid
bias issues (Shi et al., 2020) and we aim to accommodate here.
In this paper, we focus on the deterministic regime but, indeed, non-deterministic regimes
(Robins, 2004) can be naturally accommodated. Specifically, allowing non-deterministic regimes
will not change the quantity of interest, V0, and similar bias issues arise when classical inference is
applied to the non-deterministic regime under nonregularity as indicated in the development and
proof of Theorem 1. In addition, we consider a problem setting of the single time point case; i.e.,
optimal treatment regime, here for the sake of simplicity, but the proposed method can be naturally
extended to the multiple time point case; i.e., dynamic treatment regime, which can be found in
Section B of the supplementary material.
To facilitate the presentation of this paper, we let bF denote an estimator based on the full dataset
On and bFS denote the same estimator which is nevertheless based on a subset S ⊂[n] := {1, . . . , n}.
Let |S| denote the size of the set S and Sc denote the complement of S in [n]. Let zη be the upper
6

η-quantile of standard normal distribution. For any two sequences {an} , {bn}, an ≍bn implies
that there exist some universal constants c, C > 0 such that cbn ≤an ≤Cbn, an ≍p bn implies
P(cbn ≤an ≤Cbn) →1 as n →∞, an ≺bn implies an/bn →0 as n →∞and an ≺p bn implies
an/bn →p 0 as n →∞.
2.2
Bias under Nonregularity

However, under nonregularity where P(τ(X) = 0) > 0; i.e., there exists a non-negligible sub-
group that the treatments have no differences, the plug-in estimator bV0 fails to deliver valid and
optimal inference on the mean outcome under OTR. This is because the estimated OTR, bdI, in
Eq. (4) is inconsistent when the OTR is not uniquely defined. Specifically, under nonregularity,
even when the estimated contrast function bτI converges to the contrast function τ, after being
discretized to {0, 1}, the estimated decision function of OTR, bdI(X) := I{bτI(X) > 0}, might not
converge to the OTR, dopt
0
:= I{τ(X) > 0} due to the nonsmoothness of the indicator function
I{· > 0} as illustrated in Example 1 at the end of Section A.1 in the supplementary materials.
To address the inconsistency issue, a natural idea is to approximate the value function of the
OTR dopt
0 , an indicator function, by the value functions of a sequence of (pseudo) smoothing decision
functions.
To enable such approximations, we consider Assumption 5 which characterizes the
complexity of the neighbourhood of the contrast function τ(X) around 0 and allows nonregularity.
Assumption 5 (Assumption (A5) in Shi et al. (2020)) Assume there exist some positive constants
¯c, α and δ such that P (0 < |τ (X)| < t) ≤¯ctα, ∀0 < t ≤δ.
Assumption 5 is a classical margin condition in nonregular inference (Audibert and Tsybakov,
2007; Luedtke and van der Laan, 2016; Shi et al., 2020), where a smaller α indicates a looser bound
on the density of τ(X) near zero. In particular, α < 1 accommodates distributions with bounded
or even polynomially unbounded density, including but not limited to uniform, normal and beta
distributions. The specific value of α is not needed in our method.

2.3
Warm-up: Smoothing Decision Function
Let Ij be a given bipartition of n patients and Ij,k be a given bipartition of Ij for j, k = 1, 2. We
consider the smoothing estimator

ds(X; bτI, hn, t0) := I{bτI(X) ∈(−t0hn, (1 −t0)hn)} ·

where hn controls the smoothness of ds and ensures the consistency and t0 ∈(0, 1) determines the
asymmetry of ds as illustrated in Fig. 1. The local nature of ds characterized by hn ensures zero
approximation error for observations with |bτ(X)| bounded below by hn, a property that distin-
guishes the proposed local smoothing from the global smoothing (Levis et al., 2025) and is more
compatible with the margin condition in Assumption 5. The asymmetric extent of ds characterized
by t0 helps distinguish the proposed method from the other smoothing (Wu and Wang, 2021; Chen
et al., 2023; Whitehouse et al., 2025), which typically focuses on a single smoothing parameter. As
detailed in Sections 2.4 and 3, the choice of t0 does not impact validity but plays a critical role in
optimizing efficiency, which existing methods fail to achieve due to the lack of such an additional
degree of freedom in smoothing.
Note that the smoothing decision function ds is only used for inference on the mean outcome of
OTR. To estimate OTR, one should directly apply existing OTR estimation approaches instead of
using ds as ds is not an appropriately defined decision rule with mapping space {0, 1}. In addition,
besides the linear smoothing in Fig. 1, other shapes of ds, such as a quadratic one, are possible
but given the smoothing scale hn and the asymmetric extent t0, ds of any (smoothing) shape is
asymptotically equivalent as implied by Lemma A.1 of the supplementary material. Therefore,
we consider a simple form of linear smoothing here to help simplify the implementation, which
nevertheless would not sacrifice validity or efficiency of the proposed method.
To facilitate our theoretical analysis, we allow the smoothing tuning parameter hn,Ij to be

Diagram comparing the naive indicator rule and the proposed smoothed decision function as functions of the estimated treatment contrast. The naive rule jumps from zero to one when the contrast crosses zero. The smoothed rule increases linearly from zero to one over an interval of width h sub n, extending from minus t sub zero times h sub n to (one minus t sub zero) times h sub n, and is constant outside this interval. The parameter t sub zero determines the asymmetry of the interval around zero.

the decision function ds is undersmoothed and is inconsistent; e.g. hn,Ij = 0 corresponds to the
plug-in decision function ds = bd. When hn,Ij is too large, the decision function ds is oversmoothed

of the smoothing tuning parameter hn,Ij as stated in Assumptions 6 and 7. Assumption 6 requires
that hn,Ij cannot be too small in order to make sure the proposed smoothing decision function ds
is consistent, and Assumption 7 requires that hn,Ij cannot be too large in order to make sure the
difference between the value function of ds and that of the OTR, dopt

Theorem 1 establishes the asymptotic normality of our proposed smoothing estimator bVs and
justifies the use of the the two-sided level-η confidence intervals:

bVs −zη/2 · bσs
√n, bVs + zη/2 · bσs
√n

(11)
in inferring V0 regardless of whether the regularity assumption is satisfied or not. From Theorem
1, we see that the asymptotic variance of bVs consists of two parts, the regular part which is
unavoidable and the nonregular part which depends on t0. This implies that the smoothing tuning
parameter hn,Ij will not affect the asymptotic efficiency, while the asymmetric tuning parameter
t0 will. Therefore, a natural question to ask is whether we can minimize the variance by adjusting
t0 adaptively, and if yes, how. The details of the adaptive smoothing estimator are provided in the
coming subsection.
2.4
Proposed Method: Adaptive Smoothing
Built on the smoothing estimator, we introduce the proposed method, adaptive smoothing, for
inference on V0, the mean outcome under OTR. The key element of the proposed method is an
adaptive adjustment of the asymmetric tuning parameter t0 to minimize the variance, or equiva-
lently, optimize the efficiency in the smoothing estimator bVs in Eq. (7). For simplicity, we focus
on the case of homoscedasticity that conditional on the baseline covariate X, the outcome un-
der treatment and that under control have the same variance with probability tending to 1 as
stated in Assumption 8, while similar optimal method and theory can be established in the general
case allowing heteroscedasticity and the details are included in Section A.12 and Section D of the
supplementary material.
Assumption 8 (Homoscedasticity) P (Var(Y |X, A = 1) = Var(Y |X, A = 0)) = 1.
Under Assumption 8, let σ2(X) := Var(Y |X, A = 1) = Var(Y |X, A = 0) denote this common
conditional variance, then the nonregular part of the asymptotic variance of the smoothing estimator

The intuitive explanation of why replacing the fixed t0 with the estimated propensity score helps
improve efficiency is that the effective sample size in the influence function ψ depends on Ads +
(1 −A)(1 −ds) positively (Thomassen et al., 2024), so when the propensity score becomes larger,
i.e., more patients receive the treatment, a left-shifted smoothing decision function ds can better
utilize the treated patients and increase the effective sample size. The extent of the asymmetry
should be proportional to the propensity score as illustrated in Figure 2. Based on the adaptive
smoothing estimator, the proposed method for inference on the mean outcome under OTR, V0, is
summarized in Algorithm 1. Note that compared with existing methods allowing nonregularity,
such as m-out-of-n bootstrap (Chakraborty et al., 2010, 2014), online one step estimator (Luedtke
and van der Laan, 2016) and subbagging (Shi et al., 2020), Algorithm 1 is far more efficient in terms
of the computational time, as it does not require extensive resampling or refitting of the working
models.
To justify the proposed method in Algorithm 1, we note that Theorem 1 is not directly appli-
cable. First, the propensity score π(1, X) typically depends on X, while Theorem 1 is derived for
a fixed t0 only. Fortunately, we can show that Theorem 1 holds uniformly w.r.t. t0 ∈[c, 1 −c] for
any given constant c > 0, which, together with the overlap condition for the propensity score in
Assumption 3, facilitates the justification of the proposed method. Second, because the propensity

Two
-panel diagram showing the proposed adaptive smoothing decision function for two estimated propensity scores. The horizontal axis is the estimated treatment contrast and the vertical axis is the decision weight. In both panels, the naive rule jumps from zero to one at zero. The smoothed rule increases linearly from zero to one over an interval of width h sub n, from minus the estimated propensity times h sub n to (one minus the estimated propensity) times h sub n. Its value at zero equals the estimated propensity. Panel a shows a lower propensity, whereas panel b shows a higher propensity, which shifts the smoothing interval to the left.

propensity score and (b) a larger propensity score.
score π(1, X) is usually unknown in practice, we use its estimated value; i.e., bπ(1, X), instead in bVas,
and the difference between π(1, X) and bπ(1, X) might be nonnegligible and break the asymptotic
analysis in Theorem 1. Fortunately, because for the subjects whose τ(X) is around zero, bτ(X)
is also close to zero, the error induced by the estimation of the propensity score is controlled by
bτ(X)·|bπ(1, X)−π(1, X)| and is negligible under Assumption 4. Therefore, compared with Theorem
1, no additional assumptions are required to justify the proposed method. The establishment of the
asymptotic normality and thus the validity of bVas is stated in Theorem 2. The efficiency optimality
of bVas and the variance reduction over bVs and the current state-of-the-art will be given in Section
3.

2.5
Tuning Selection and Practical Implementation
In this subsection, we introduce a data-driven approach to select the smoothing tuning parameter
hn,Ij (j = 1, 2) in practice. The proposed method guarantees that the selected hn,Ij falls into
the theoretical range required in Assumptions 6 and 7 with probability tending to 1.
The
key idea of the proposed tuning approach is to construct a sample-split-based statistic, the
estimated approximation error (EAE) of bτIj, that approximates the order of the lower bound
n1/4  E[|bτIj(X) −τ(X)|2]

2(1+α) . Specifically, to prevent the
selected hn,Ij from being too small in finite sample, we consider a truncation (log(n)/(C0n)) for
the estimated approximation error where C0 is a constant. Additional simulation results in Section
A.11 of the supplementary material demonstrate that the tuning selection remains relatively
robust across different choices of C0, and for practical use, we suggest C0 is chosen between 0.01
and 0.05. Details of the tuning selection of hn,Ij are summarized in Algorithm 2.

order as the lower bound rather than minimize MSE. This is fundamentally different from typical
nonparametric estimation problems where undersmoothing or bandwidth optimization is needed.

3
Efficiency Optimality
In this section, we establish the efficiency optimality of the proposed adaptive smoothing approach
in Algorithm 1. Specifically, we first introduce the challenge of developing an efficiency optimality
theory for inference on the mean outcome under OTR, V0, when nonregularity is allowed. Then,
we propose the Robust Asymptotically Linear Unbiased estimator class, and discuss the relevance
16


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 17 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 17.
and generalizablity of the proposed class. In the end, we demonstrate the efficiency optimality of
our proposed adaptive smoothing method by deriving a lower bound on the asymptotic variance for
our proposed estimator class and showing that our proposed adaptive smoothing estimator belongs
to the proposed estimator class and achieves its asymptotic variance lower bound.
3.1
Challenges of Deriving Optimality under Nonregularity
Although semiparametric optimality has been well established for inference on the mean outcome
under OTR under regularity, establishing efficiency optimality under nonregularity is well-known to
be challenging due to the lack of path-wise differentiability (van der Laan and Rose, 2011; Luedtke
and van der Laan, 2016).
This is because in semiparametric theory, the concept of a regular
estimator is only well-defined when the target parameter is pathwise differentiable (Bickel et al.,
1993; Van der Vaart, 2000). Specifically, the semiparametric efficiency bound is established within
the regular estimator for OTR which requires the map from the distribution of (X, A, Y ) to the
quantity of interest V0 is path-wise differentiable (Kennedy, 2017; Bickel et al., 1993). As shown in
Luedtke and van der Laan (2016), the path-wise differentiability of the mean outcome under OTR,
V0, is indeed equivalent to
P

X : τ(X)̸ = 0 ∨max
A∈{0,1} Var(Y |A, X) = 0

= 1,
(17)
which is naturally satisfied under regularity; i.e., P(τ(X) = 0) = 0. However, under nonregularity;
i.e., P(τ(X) = 0) > 0, the path-wise differentiability of V0, or equivalently, Eq. (17), is satisfied
if and only if for A = 0, 1, Var(Y |A, X) = 0 a.e. for X where τ(X) = 0, which requires given A
and X, Y is a constant for the patients in the nonregular set {X : τ(X) = 0} and is clearly an
unrealistic condition. Therefore, under nonregularity that P(τ(X) = 0) > 0, the regular estimator
for V0 cannot be well defined, not to mention deriving semiparametric optimality.
To establish efficiency optimality for inference on V0 when nonregularity is allowed, we need to
consider a new estimator class which bears the following properties: (1) its definition remains valid
under nonregularity; (2) it should be general enough to include the proposed estimator and common
estimators considered in both regular and nonregular case and (3) the variance bound exists and is
meaningful. Considering all these criteria, one class naturally comes to mind is the asymptotically
17


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 18 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 18.
linear estimator, as its definition only depends on linearity, and is free of path-wise differentiability
and is valid under nonregularity. Moreover, the asymptotically linear estimator includes not only
the proposed adaptive smoothing estimator bVas but also efficient regular estimators (Bickel et al.,
1993), the most efficient ones among regular estimators for V0, which we denote by bVer. However,
deriving a variance lower bound for asymptotically linear estimators is well recognized as impossible
because the class is too broad (Van der Vaart, 2000). Therefore, appropriate constraints are needed
for the class of asymptotically linear estimators to derive efficiency optimality for inference on the
mean outcome under OTR, V0, when nonregularity is allowed.
3.2
Robust Asymptotically Linear Unbiased Estimator
Motivated by the discussion of the asymptotically linear estimator in Section 3.1, we propose the
robust asymptotically linear unbiased estimator class, TRL, for the mean outcome under OTR, V0,
as defined in Definition 1. Here, “robust” refers to double robustness and inclusion of ra is to ensure
that the class TRL is rich enough to accommodate heteroscedasticity case as detailed in Sections
A.12 and D of the supplementary material. In Definition 1, we impose no restrictions on the working
models µa, π1 and ra, which may or may not coincide with their population truth. The essential
constraint is on the function f, which requires the doubly robust condition that EP [f] = V0 holds
whenever either the outcome model or the propensity score is correctly specified. Such doubly
robust constraints on the influence function are widely adopted in observational studies (Ding,
2024).
Definition 1 (Robust Asymptotically Linear Unbiased Estimator) Let c ∈(0, 1/2) be a fixed
constant and let P denote the set of distributions satisfying Assumptions 1 to 3. The class TRL is
the set of estimators Tn for which there exists a measurable function f : X × {0, 1} × R3 × (c, 1 −
c) × {−1, 0, 1} × R2
≥0 →R such that, for every P ∈P,

(18)

for EP [Y | X, A = a], EP [A | X] and VarP (Y | X, A = a), respectively, for a = 0, 1. Moreover, for

3.3
Efficiency Optimality
Under the same assumptions of Theorem 2 and some mild conditions, Theorem 3 derives the
variance lower bound of the proposed robust asymptotically linear unbiased class TRL and shows
that the proposed adaptive smoothing estimator bVas achieves the variance lower bound of TRL.
The correct specification of the working models in Theorem 3 is analogous to the requirement in
classical semiparametric efficiency bounds that all nuisance functions need to be correctly specified
in the Convolution Theorem (Bickel et al., 1993). This is mainly because under misspecification,
inference validity is not guaranteed in general and optimizing efficiency without validity is not
meaningful (Tsiatis, 2006).
Theorem 3 (Efficiency Optimality of bVas) Under the same assumptions of Theorem 2, when the
working models µ1, µ0 and π1 in Tn are correctly specified, and Tn satisfies asymptotic uniform
integrability, i.e., limM→∞supn E
h
n (Tn −V0)2 · 1


i
= 0, we have
inf

n→∞Var(√nbVas)
= Var

.
The efficiency optimality of bVas established in Theorem 3 within TRL is due to the special hidden
structure of TRL. Specifically, by variational principle, we can show that the estimator in TRL can be
decomposed into one part asymptotically equivalent to bVas and the other part uncorrelated with bVas.
20


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 21 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 21.
Then, by variance decomposition, we can show that bVas exactly attains the variance lower bound
of TRL. This is substantially different from the convolution theorem of classical semiparametric
efficiency optimality (Bickel et al., 1993).
Three implications from Theorem 3 are worth noting, as all the estimators in Corollary 1 have
correctly specified working models of µ1, µ0 and π1 under the assumptions therein in order to ensure
their inference validity. First, because the current state-of-the-art estimator bVsb belongs to TRL, the
proposed adaptive smoothing estimator bVas dominates all existing methods allowing nonregularity,
with a significant variance reduction over subbagging bVsb stated in Corollary 2. Second, because the
proposed smoothing estimator bVs belongs to TRL, the variance reduction of the proposed adaptive
one bVas over bVs is always nonnegative no matter what t0 is chosen and is indeed strict unless
P(π(1, X) = t0 ∨τ(X)̸ = 0) = 1; the proof is in Section A.10 of the supplementary material. Third,
because the efficient regular estimator belongs to TRL, under regularity, bVas is asymptotically
equivalent to the efficient regular estimator and achieves the semiparametric efficiency bound,
to which the derived variance bound in Theorem 3 indeed reduces. From this perspective, the
optimality established by Definition 1 and Theorem 3 encompasses classical semiparametric theories
in inference on the mean outcome under OTR, and bVas achieves optimal efficiency in both regular
and nonregular cases.
Corollary 2 (Variance Reduction over Subbagging) Under the same assumptions of Theorem 3,
and assumptions of Theorem 3 in Shi et al. (2020), we have

4
Simulation Studies
In this section, we study the finite sample performance of the proposed method via Monte Carlo
simulation in the single time point case. To start with, we consider a data generating model similar
21


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 22 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 22.
to that in Shi et al. (2020), i.e., Y = Φ (X1, X2) + Aτ (X1, X2) + e, where the random error term
e ∼Normal(0, 0.52) is independent of A, X1 and X2. We vary the generation of X1 and X2, the
baseline function Φ, the contrast function τ and the propensity score π, and consider five different
scenarios (A)-(E) including both regular cases and nonregular cases as summarized in Table 1. In
all scenarios, X1 and X2 are independent, and the mean outcome under OTR, V0, can be explicitly
calculated as shown in Table 1. In Scenarios (A), (C) and (E), the OTR is not uniquely defined; i.e.,
nonregular, since the contrast functions in these scenarios satisfy P (τ (X1, X2) = 0) = P (X1 = 0),
which equals 0.6, 0.7 and 0.9, respectively. On the contrary, in Scenarios (B) and (D), we have
P (τ (X1, X2) = 0) = 0; i.e., regular. For each scenario, we let the sample size n = 1000, and
comparison is made among the following methods:
• Proposed: confidence interval obtained from adaptive smoothing combined with repeatedly
cross fitting and averaging (details in Algorithm 3 in the supplementary material), and hn,Ij’s
selected by Algorithm 2.
• Subbagging: confidence interval of subbagging in Shi et al. (2020).
• SSS: confidence interval by simply splitting sample into two parts and using half to estimate
the decision function, propensity score and the conditional mean functions, and the other half
to evaluate the plug-in estimator.
In Scenarios (A) and (B), we apply nonparametric maximum likelihood estimators to estimate
the propensity score π and the conditional mean function h. In Scenarios (C)-(E), we apply the
cubic spline to estimate π and h, and use the same truncation on the estimation of π as that in
Shi et al. (2020) (with the lower bound of 0.05 and upper bound 0.95). In this simulation, we
let C0 = 0.05 for the tuning selection in Algorithm 2 and subsample size K0 = 5 in subbaging.
The resampling times B for all the methods are 1000, and the observed results including Empirical
Coverage Probability (ECP) and Average Length of confidence intervals (AL) are aggregated over
500 Monte Carlo samples.
From Table 2, we see that the proposed method achieves the nominal coverage probability;
i.e., 95%, well in both regular, Scenarios (B) and (D), and nonregular cases, Scenarios (A), (C)
and (E), which is in accordance with the asymptotic theory in Theorem 2. The results in Table 2
22


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 23 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 23.
demonstrate that in all the scenarios regardless of whether it is regular or not, the proposed method
has shortest confidence interval among all the competing methods. In particular, in the nonregular
cases, Scenarios (A), (C) and (E), the efficiency gain of the proposed method over the current state-
of-the-art method, subbagging, is significant with about 10%-25% reduction of average length from
the proposed method over that from subbaging, which is consistent with the efficiency optimality
in Theorem 3.
We also conduct additional simulations to demonstrate (1) the robustness w.r.t. the constant
C0 in Algorithm 2; (2) the robustness w.r.t. the number of the split in the proposed method and (3)
the computational benefit of the proposed method in Section A.11, and (4) the robustness of the
proposed method w.r.t. sample size under heteroscedasticity and more challenging settings, e.g.,
α < 1 and multi-dimensional covariates, in Section A.12 of the supplementary material. Simulation
results demonstrating the merit of the proposed method in multiple time point cases are also
included in Section B.4 and Section D of the supplementary material.
5
Real Data Analysis
In this section, we apply the proposed method to the data from AIDS Clinical Trials Group Pro-
tocol 175 (ACTG175) which is a randomized clinical trial comparing the treatment effects of two
monotherapies, zidovudine (ZDV) and didanosine (ddI), and two combination therapies, ZDV+ddI
and ZDV+zalcitabine (zal), for patients infected with the human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)
and of CD4 T cell counts between 200 and 500 per cubic millimeter (Hammer et al., 1996). Here,
we focus on a subset of 1046 patients who receive the treatment, ZDV + ddI, denoted by A = 1 or
the control, ZDV + zal, denoted by A = 0. We choose age as the covariate X as suggested in Fan
et al. (2017), consider the CD4 T cell count at 20+/-5 weeks as the response Y , and compare the
performance of the proposed method in Algorithm 3 with C0 = 0.01 and the subbagging proposed
in Shi et al. (2020) with K0 = 4.
From Table 3, we see that the length of the 95% confidence interval from the proposed method is
shorter than that from the subbagging, which is consistent to Theorem 3 and the simulation results.
The length reduction is about 5%, and such a significant reduction implies that nonregularity might
exist in this study, which is in accordance with previous findings in Fan et al. (2017) and Shi et al.
23


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 24 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 24.
(2020). Here, the length reduction is smaller than that in simulation, which is due to the relatively
balanced design in ACTG175 (Hammer et al., 1996) as discussed in Eq.
(22).
The efficiency
gain with the proposed method also helps generate statistical evidence in a more trustworthy and
powerful way. Consider a toy example where we test the null hypothesis that the average difference
of the CD4 T cell count under OTR is 387.
Then, the proposed method can reject the null
hypothesis while the subbagging can not under the significance level 0.05.
6
Discussion
Statistical inference on the mean outcome under OTR plays an essential role in precision medicine,
and how to address challenges brought by potential nonregularity in the inference is a critical ques-
tion in drug development and regulatory decision-making. In this paper, we propose an adaptive
smoothing approach to infer the mean outcome under OTR regardless of whether the regularity as-
sumption is satisfied or not. The proposed method is valid, easy-to-compute and efficiency-optimal
within the robust asymptotically linear unbiased estimators. Revisiting the ACTG 175 trial, we
not only show that the proposed method can lead to more trustworthy statistical findings in a
more efficient way, but also demonstrate that before any statistical inference is made on the mean
outcome under OTR, we must appropriately account for potential nonregularity.
Theoretically, this paper paves a novel way to establish efficiency optimality theories for OTR
in the general scenario allowing nonregularity via variational principle. In particular, we derive a
variance lower bound for the class of robust asymptotically linear unbiased estimators which is free
of path-wise differentiability required in classical semiparametric theories and can be well defined
under nonregularity in OTR. Under regularity, the proposed class includes the efficient regular
estimators and the derived variance lower bound reduces to the classical semiparametric efficiency
bound in inference on the mean outcome under OTR.
In this paper, we focus on the binary OTR without cost constraints. However, several more
complex scenarios of OTR are also of interest in practice. First, the OTR might be defined with
cost constraints as in precision medicine, we sometimes need to strike a balance between the benefit
and the cost (Illenberger et al., 2023). Second, the OTR might be considered when the treatment
is of continuous values (Ai et al., 2026). Third, the OTR may be restricted to a parametric class,
24


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 25 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 25.
such as linear index rules, that might be misspecified and induce additional bias (Wu and Wang,
2021). When nonregularity is allowed, how to extend the proposed adaptive smoothing approach
and efficiency optimality theories to infer the mean outcome under constrained OTR, continuous
OTR and parametric OTR requires further investigation.
Data Availability
The ACTG 175 data analysed in this article are publicly available as the ACTG175 data set in
the R package speff2trial, version 1.0.5, through the Comprehensive R Archive Network at
https://CRAN.R-project.org/package=speff2trial.

SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL
In Appendix A, we present additional details of the single time point case, including the proofs of all
the theoretical results in the single time point case, the sensitivity analysis of tuning selection, and
the extensions of the methodological and theoretical results to the case of heteroscedasticity together
with additional simulation studies examining heteroscedasticity, α < 1 and multi-dimensional co-
variates. In Appendix B, we present the setting, methodology, theoretical results and simulation
studies in the multiple time point case. In Appendix C, we present proofs of all the theoretical
results in the multiple time point case. In Appendix D, we extend the methodological and theoret-
ical results in the multiple time point case to the case of heteroscedasticity and provide additional
simulation studies.
A
Additional Details and Proofs of Theoretical Results in Single
Time Point Case
In this section, we focus on the theoretical results in the single time point case.
A.1
Asymptotic Normality for Plug-in Estimator in Regular Case
For the inverse-weighted terms below, we also use a fixed fitted-overlap condition (Chernozhukov
et al., 2018; Shi et al., 2020), consistent with the common practice of bounding or truncating fitted
propensity scores to control extreme inverse weights (Kang and Schafer, 2007; Tan, 2010; Ju et al.,
2019): for some constant cf ∈(0, 1/2), with probability tending to one over the training data,
bπI(a, X) ∈(cf, 1 −cf) PX-a.s. for a = 0, 1 and all fitted folds I. The corresponding condition is
used for the stage-specific fitted propensity scores in the multiple time point case, and cf need not
be known.
Proof. The justification is mainly based on Assumption 1 to 4 in the main text and the fitted-overlap
condition above. In specific, recall that

However, in the nonregular case, the naive cross-fitted estimator does not work due to the
inconsistency of the estimated decision rule, as is illustrated in Example 1 below.
Example 1 Assume there are 400 patients with 200 males and 200 females randomly assigned
with treatment and control. The potential outcomes are defined as follows, (1) outcomes of female
patients under treatment follow N(1, 1); (2) outcomes of the others follow N(0, 1). Individualized
treatment effect is estimated by difference in means estimator, i.e.,
bτ(G) =
Pn
i=1 YiI{Xi = G, A = 1}
Pn
i=1 I{Xi = G, A = 1} −
Pn
i=1 YiI{Xi = G, A = 0}
Pn
i=1 I{Xi = G, A = 0} ,
G ∈{male, female}.
(42)
It is clear that there exists nonregularity in this toy example as for male patients, the treatment
and the control have no differences. Figure 4 illustrates the bias issue under nonregularity. Figure
36


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 37 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 37.
5 shows that how the consistency of the estimated decision function is broken under nonregularity
and fixed by smoothing.
Figure 4: The boxplot of the plug-in estimator in Eq. (4) of the mean outcome under OTR in
Example 1 where the red line is the mean outcome under the true OTR.
Figure 5: The left panel is bτ(male), the middle panel is bd(male), and the right panel is ds(male) as
sample size increases in Example 1.
A.2
Lemma A.1 and Its Proof
Define the following class of decision functions:
D(V0) := {D|D : τ(X) →[0, 1] ∧E(ψ(D(τ(X0)), π, h)) = V0}.
where the expectation is equal to V0. For the purpose of generalization and to cover as many
situations as possible, we consider a sequence of decision function {Dn}n∈N+ (X × R →[0, 1]) with
the following properties.
Assumption 10 (Uniform continuity) ∃δn s.t. PX(|bτI(X) −τ(X)| ≥δn) = op(1) and
sup
X∈X,|s−t|≤δn

All the above assumptions are set to guarantee the sequence of decision function Dn(·, bτI(·)) (I is
certain subset of [n]) converges to D(·) ∈D(V0) in an uniform and fast enough manner. Assumption
10 requires the smoothness of Dn, ensuring the sequence of decision function Dn(·, bτI(·)) being
convergent. Assumption 11 indicates that the sequence Dn is meaningful for the task of inferring
V0 as the limit is OTR. Assumption 12 suggests that Dn(·, s) should converge to I{s > 0} in a
faster enough rate when s is away from zero in the same side, thus ensures the convergence rate
from V (Dn(X, bτI(X))) to V0 is o(n−1/2). Assumptions 10 to 12 can be implied by Assumption 6
and 7 when we justify Theorems 1 and 2.

A.5
Additional Conditions and Proof of Proposition 1
Throughout this subsection, EX denotes expectation over a fresh draw of X, with the training data
fixed. A fold I is one of the half or quarter folds used by Algorithm 2, and every such fold has size
of order n.
Condition (C1) (near-parametric rate). EX|bτI(X) −τ(X)|2 = Op(log n/n) for every such
fold.
48


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 49 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 49.
Condition (C2) (stochastic boundedness). ∥τ∥∞< ∞and ∥bτI∥∞= Op(1) for every such
fold.
Condition (C3) (variability among splits). For any two disjoint such folds I, I′ with |I| ≍
|I′| ≍n,
EX|bτI(X) −bτI′(X)|2 ≍p EX|bτI(X) −τ(X)|2.
Condition (C1) requires the near-parametric rate EX[(bτI(X) −τ(X))2] = Op(log n/n), which
correctly specified parametric working models attain. When the working model satisfies the rate
requirement in Condition (C1), the truncation term log(n)/(C0n) of Algorithm 2 becomes the
leading term. Therefore, the desired range of the tuning parameter is satisfied automatically by
Algorithm 2 because the truncation term already dominates the estimation error and is of order
sufficiently smaller than that of the desired upper bound by Assumption 9.
Condition (C2) requires the estimators to be stochastically bounded, which is a standard as-
sumption in the causal inference literature. In practice, Condition (C2) can also be enforced by
truncating bτI at a known bound. Under Conditions (C2) and (C3), Condition (C2) provides con-
ditional concentration of the two validation-fold averages entering the EAE, and Condition (C3)
links their population counterparts to the estimation error. Under Condition (C1), the truncation
term log(n)/(C0n) yields the comparison in Eq. (66) directly.
Condition (C3) requires the average squared difference between two independently trained es-
timators, which is what the EAE estimates, to be of the same order as the estimation error.
Condition (C3) is mild, and for two folds of the same size, averaging over the training data of both
sides of Condition (C3), we can show that Condition (C3) basically asks the squared bias of the
estimator not to dominate the order of its variance on average
EX

as the other direction ≳holds trivially. Lemma A.2 covers correctly specified parametric work-
ing models. Lemma A.3 gives high-level sufficient conditions for undersmoothed estimators, and
Examples 2 and 3 verify those conditions for explicit spline and kernel estimators. Section A.11
illustrates the condition numerically.

smoothers.

Example 3 (Undersmoothed kernel contrast) Let Tp = [0, 1]p be the p-dimensional torus. Suppose
the observations in every training fold are i.i.d. copies of (X, Z) satisfying

A.6
Algorithm 3 and Its Property
Algorithm 3 presents a refinement of the proposed adaptive smoothing method in the manuscript by

3 and suggests the asymptotic variance remains unchanged.

A.8
Extensions of TRL
With some slight modifications to replace the linear i.i.d. summation with linear martingale sum-
mation, we can extend TRL, the proposed robust asymptotically linear unbiased estimator class,
to a more general version containing simple sample split estimator (Belloni et al., 2014; Wager
and Athey, 2018) and online one step estimator (Luedtke and van der Laan, 2016) as shown in
Definition 2.
Definition 2 (Martingale Robust Asymptotically Linear Unbiased Estimator) The class TMRL
is defined as the set of the estimator Tm (m ≤n) such that there exists a measurable function
f(·|Oi−1) : X × {0, 1} × R3 × (c, 1 −c) × {−1, 0, 1} × R2
≥0 and for all P ∈P which is the set of
distributions satisfying Assumptions 1 to 3,
Tm(Om) = m−1
m
X
i=1
f(Xi, Ai, Yi, µ1(Xi), µ0(Xi), π1(Xi), sgn (τP (Xi)) ,
r1(Xi), r0(Xi); Oi−1) + op(m−1/2)
where τP (X) = EP [Y |X, A = 1] −EP [Y |X, A = 0], and µa, π1 and ra are the working models for
EP [Y |X, A = a], EP [A|X] and VarP (Y |X, A = a), respectively, for a = 0, 1. Moreover, for any
74


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 75 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 75.
P ∈P, f satisfies
EP [f|Oi−1] = EP [EP {Y |X, A = dopt(X)}]
(96)
as long as one of the following equations holds P-a.s.:
EP [Y |X, A] =Aµ1(X) + (1 −A)µ0(X), EP [A|X] = π1(X).
(97)
Simple sample split estimator belongs to TMRL when m = |I|, as i.i.d. is a special case of such
a martingale sequence. The proof of Theorem 2 in Luedtke and van der Laan (2016) has already
implied that the online one step estimator belongs to TMRL. Note that the variance lower bound
of TMRL is the same as that of TRL, which can be shown directly by replacing the f(·) in the proof
of Theorem A.1 with f(·; Oi−1) in TMRL.

A.11
Sensitivity Analysis of Tuning
Here, we provide simulation results to demonstrate the robustness of the algorithms w.r.t. the
constant C0 in the proposed tuning selection method in Algorithm 2 and the number of the split;
i.e. Algorithm 1 (one split) and Algorithm 3 (repeated split), and illustrate the computational
benefit of the proposed method in Algorithm 1 (one split). The simulation settings are the same
as that in Section 4 of the main text.
By comparing the results in Table 4 and Table 5, we see that (1) the ECP and AL for both
Algorithm 1 and Algorithm 3 are robust to the choice of the constant C0; (2) the performances in
terms of ECP and AL of Algorithm 1 (one split) and Algorithm 3 (repeated split) are comparable
and almost the same which indicates the proposed method is robust to the number of the split;
(3) the computational time of Algorithm 1 is much shorter than that of Algorithm 3 and that of
subbagging in Shi et al. (2020), the current state-of-the-art method, in Table 6, as Algorithm 1 does
not require repeatedly sample splitting, thus avoiding multiple training of the working models.

Numerical check of the tuning conditions. We conduct simulation and report two quantities,
the variability ratio and the truncated tracking ratio, to numerically verify the commonly used
parametric and undersmoothing estimators satisfy the desired tuning selection condition and prop-
erty as summarized in Table 7. Specifically, the variability ratio illustrates Condition (C3), and
the truncated tracking ratio illustrates the approximation of the order of the estimated contrast
function by EAE after truncation. We consider the correctly specified parametric working model
and undersmoothed kernel and spline estimators of the same types used in the simulation and
application of the manuscript. The results support that the additional conditions are mild in these
settings and illustrate the finite-sample behavior of Algorithm 2.
Specifically, we use X ∼Uniform(0, 1), A ∼Bernoulli(1/2), and Y = µ0(X)+Aτ(X)+ε, where
µ0(x) = sin(2x)+x2 and ε ∼N(0, 0.25). We take n ∈{500, 2000, 8000}, with 300 replications for the
first two sample sizes and 150 for the last. The parametric design uses τ(x) = 1−2x and a correctly
specified linear interaction model.
The nonparametric design uses τ(x) = x sin(3x) −0.3 and
separate within-arm regressions: quadratic splines with ⌊1.2|I|1/3⌋interior knots on a truncated-
power basis, undersmoothed relative to the |I|1/5-optimal choice, or a Nadaraya–Watson estimator
with a Gaussian kernel and bandwidth 0.8|I|−1/3, which is also undersmoothed.
78


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 79 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 79.
The sample is split as in Algorithms 1 and 2 into half folds I1, I2 and quarter folds Ij,k,
j, k ∈{1, 2}, with |Ij| = n/2. For each k, the half-fold fit trained on Ij is compared with the
quarter-fold fit trained on I3−j,3−k, and their squared difference is evaluated on I3−j,k. Population
L2(PX) norms are approximated on a grid of 1,500 points. The table reports two ratios for each
estimator and each n:
• The variability ratio is
EX|bτI3−j,1(X) −τ(X)|2
EX|bτI3−j,1(X) −bτI3−j,2(X)|2 .
It is bounded above and below exactly when the two sides of Condition (C3) are of the same
order on average, and it equals 1/2 when the error has no bias.
• The truncated tracking ratio is
EAE ∨(log n/n)
EX|bτIj(X) −τ(X)|2 ∨(log n/n).
This ratio is the finite-sample analogue of the comparison used in the proof, and under the
stated sufficient conditions, it should remain bounded away from 0 and ∞.

A.12
Extensions to Heteroscedasticity in Single Time Point Case
In the single time point case allowing heteroscedasticity, we can show that the optimal efficiency
can be achieved by bVD where
D(X) = I{τ(X) > 0} +
Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)
Var(Y |X, A = 1)π(0, X) + Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)I{τ(X) = 0}.
(98)
The results are summarized in Theorem A.1.

Lemma A.4 Let µ ∈R and σ2 > 0 be fixed constants. If E[f(U)] is a constant C for any U s.t.
E[U] = µ and Var[U] = σ2, then f(u) is a quadratic polynomial in u.

Proof of Theorem A.1. Here, we show the results in a more general case allowing heteroscedasticity;
i.e. without Assumption 8. The sketch of this proof is as follows: (1) show that the influence

function f is also unbiased conditional on X; (2) by variational principle, show that the influence
function f must follow a standard form of being a special linear function w.r.t. Y and (3) by
variance decomposition of the standard form, show that bVas is optimal.
Firstly, we show that the unbiasedness required in Definition 1; i.e. E[f] = E[E[Y |X, A =
dopt(X)]] for any distribution of (X, A, Y ), implies conditional unbiasedness
E[f|X] = E[Y |X, A = dopt(X)]
with probability 1 for any distribution of (X, A, Y ). We show this by contradiction. Assume that
for joint distribution (X, A, Y ), we have
E[f|X]̸ = E[Y |X, A = dopt(X)]
in a positive probability. Then, one of the following two sets:
X> :={X : E[f|X] > E[Y |X, A = dopt(X)]};
X< :={X : E[f|X] < E[Y |X, A = dopt(X)]}

In the case of homoscedasticity, it just reduces to the optimal adaptive smoothing estimator we
proposed, i.e., bV ∗
D with D(X) = I{τ(X) > 0} + π(1, X)I{τ(X) = 0}.
Then, in terms of the data driven implementation of achieving the above stated optimal effi-
ciency, under the same choice hn,Ij’s and and working models of h and π, replacing the t0 in bVs
with any consistent estimator of
Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)
Var(Y |X, A = 1)π(0, X) + Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)
(125)
would lead to an estimator which is asymptotically equivalent with bVD where
D(X) = I{τ(X) > 0} +
Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)
Var(Y |X, A = 1)π(0, X) + Var(Y |X, A = 0)π(1, X)I{τ(X) = 0},
and hence the efficiency bound of TRL can be achieved by a practically meaningful estimator in
the case of heteroscedasticity.
Here we emphasize that the rate requirement of estimating Eq.
(125) is so mild that any consistent estimator would be satisfactory, which is exactly the meaning
of Assumption 11. Since we can already consistently estimate π by Assumption 4, it suffice to
obtain a consistent estimator for Var(Y |X, A) to achieve the efficiency optimality as described in
128. There exists several model free nonparametric estimators for such individualized variance, see
96


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 97 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 97.
an overview at Chu et al. (2020). Moreover, the additional condition of correct specification of
conditional variance is required only for efficiency optimality under heteroscedasticity. Even if it is
violated, inference validity under heteroscedasticity is still preserved, as the asymmetric extent t0
does not influence the validity. In the heteroscedasticity case, the general validity of the proposed
estimator allowing misspecification of conditional variances can be implied by Lemma A.1. We
summarize the algorithm as follows.

To provide more numerical evidence, we present seven additional simulation settings in the
single time point case that examine the proposed method across different combinations of het-
eroscedasticity, α, and covariate dimension. All seven settings, summarized in Table 8, share the
99


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 100 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 100.
data generating model
Y = Φ(X) + A τ(X1, X2) + e,
where the random error e is independent of (A, X) but its variance may depend on A and X,
introducing heteroscedasticity. In all settings, the treatment effect τ depends only on (X1, X2)
with P(τ(X1, X2) = 0) = P(X1 = 0) > 0, so nonregularity is present.
Settings (I) and (II) feature heteroscedasticity with α < 1 and X2 ∼Uniform[−2, 2]. In Set-
ting (I), heteroscedasticity arises in the nonregular subgroup: for patients with X1 = 0 where
τ = 0, the conditional variance under treatment is three times that under control, while for pa-
tients with X1 = 1 where τ > 0, the error variances are equal. In Setting (II), heteroscedasticity
enters through the continuous covariate X2: Var(Y | X, A = 1) = 0.2 + 0.3|X2| increases with |X2|,
while Var(Y | X, A = 0) = 0.2 remains constant.
Setting (III) combines heteroscedasticity with discrete covariates and α = ∞. Its structure is
similar to the main text Setting A, but the variance under control is over five times that under
treatment. Because τ(x1, x2) = 0.4 x1 takes values in {0, 0.4}, the treatment effect is either exactly
zero or bounded away from zero, yielding α = ∞.
Settings (IV) and (V) share the treatment effect τ(x1, x2) = 0.5 x1 x2 with X2 ∼Beta(0.5, 2),
which induces α = 1/2 due to the unbounded density of the Beta distribution near zero. Setting (IV)
is homoscedastic with Var(Y | X, A = a) = 0.25 for both treatment groups, while Setting (V)
introduces heteroscedasticity.
Settings (VI) and (VII) extend Settings (I) and (II) to a higher-dimensional covariate space with
p = 5. Three additional covariates X3 ∼N(0, 1), X4 ∼Uniform(0, 1), and X5 ∼Bernoulli(0.5)
enter the baseline outcome Φ and the propensity score π, but do not affect the treatment effect τ,
which remains a function of (X1, X2) only. The margin exponent, heteroscedasticity structure, and
nonregularity are identical to those in Settings (I) and (II), respectively. These settings examine
whether the proposed method remains effective when the nuisance functions involve a larger number
of covariates.
Across the seven settings, the margin exponent in Assumption 5 takes three distinct values:
α = 1/2 in Settings (I), (IV), (V), and (VI), α = 2/3 in Settings (II) and (VII), and α = ∞in
Setting (III). All settings with α < ∞correspond to the more challenging case where the density of
100


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 101 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 101.
|τ(X)| near zero is unbounded, as the functions x2
2/4 and |x2|3/2/4 vanish at x2 = 0 in Settings (I),
(II), (VI), and (VII), and 0.5 x2 with x2 ∼Beta(0.5, 2) concentrates mass near zero in Settings (IV)
and (V). This contrasts with the main text simulations where α ≥1.
For each setting, we let the sample size n ∈{500, 1000, 2000}. We compare the following four
methods:
• Proposed (Heter): adaptive smoothing with the efficiency-optimal asymmetric tuning under
heteroscedasticity as derived in Section D of the supplementary material, where t0 is set
to br0(X)bπ(1, X)/{br1(X)(1 −bπ(1, X)) + br0(X)bπ(1, X)} with bra(X) denoting the estimated
conditional variance d
Var(Y |X, A = a).
• Proposed (Homo): adaptive smoothing with the efficiency-optimal asymmetric tuning under
homoscedasticity as in Algorithm 1, where t0 = bπ(1, X).
• Subbagging: confidence interval of subbagging in Shi et al. (2020).
• SSS: confidence interval by simply splitting the sample into two halves, using one half to
estimate the decision function, propensity score, and conditional mean functions, and the
other half to evaluate the plug-in estimator.
We set C0 = 0.05 for the tuning selection in Algorithm 2 and subsample size K0 = 5 in subbagging.
All results are aggregated over 500 Monte Carlo replications.
In Settings (I), (II), (VI) and (VII), since X1 is discrete, we stratify the outcome model by X1
and estimate the conditional mean function µ(a, X) by ordinary least squares (OLS) with correctly
specified basis functions of X2 (e.g., X2
2 in Settings (I) and (VI), and |X2|3/2 in Settings (II)
and (VII)) within each (X1, A) stratum. In Settings (VI) and (VII), the additional covariates X3,
X4 and X5 enter the outcome model through their correctly specified linear terms. The propensity
score is estimated by OLS regression of A on the relevant covariates. In Setting (III), where both
covariates are discrete, the propensity score and the conditional mean functions are estimated by the
nonparametric maximum likelihood estimator, i.e., sample means within strata, the same approach
as in the main text Settings (A) and (B). In Settings (IV) and (V), where X2 ∼Beta(0.5, 2) is
continuous, the conditional mean functions are estimated using natural cubic splines with 4 degrees
of freedom for X2, interacted with the discrete covariate X1. In all settings, the propensity score
101


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 102 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 102.
is truncated to [0.05, 0.95], the contrast function is estimated as bτ(X) = bµ(1, X) −bµ(0, X), and
the bandwidth is selected by Algorithm 2 with C0 = 0.05. For the Proposed (Heter) method, the
conditional variance Var(Y | X, A = a) is estimated by a residual-based approach: after fitting
the outcome model bµ(a, X), we compute the squared residuals be2
i = (Yi −bµ(Ai, Xi))2 for subjects
with Ai = a and then either take the stratum mean (when the true variance is constant within
the stratum, e.g., Settings (I), (III) and (VI)) or regress be2
i on the relevant covariates (e.g., |X2| in
Settings (II) and (VII)), with a lower truncation of 0.05 to ensure numerical stability. The standard
deviation of bVas is computed as the sample standard deviation of the influence function values ψi
across all four folds, as in Eq. (127). Both proposed methods are implemented via Algorithm 1
(single cross-fitting) and the results are aggregated over 500 Monte Carlo replications.
The results in Table 9 reveal several findings. First, in all heteroscedastic settings (I)–(III),
(V), (VI), and (VII), Proposed (Heter) achieves the shortest confidence intervals at every sample
size, confirming the efficiency gain from correctly incorporating the variance structure into the
asymmetric tuning. In the homoscedastic Setting (IV), Proposed (Heter) and Proposed (Homo)
produce identical results, which is expected: when Var(Y | X, A = 1) = Var(Y | X, A = 0),
the variance-weighted tuning t0 reduces exactly to the propensity-based tuning, and this empirical
equivalence validates the theoretical derivation in Section A.12.
Second, the magnitude of the efficiency gain from Proposed (Heter) over Proposed (Homo)
increases with the severity of heteroscedasticity. In Settings (III) and (V), where the variance ratio
between treatment groups exceeds four, the average length reduction is approximately 13%–16%.
In Settings (I), (II), (VI), and (VII), where heteroscedasticity is more moderate, the reduction is
approximately 3%–9%.
Third, comparing Settings (VI) and (VII) (p = 5 covariates) with their counterparts Settings (I)
and (II) (p = 2), the proposed methods maintain valid coverage and comparable confidence interval
lengths. The additional covariates enter the nuisance estimation but do not materially affect the
efficiency of the value estimator, confirming the robustness of our data-driven tuning (Algorithm 2)
to the covariate dimension.
Finally, across all the settings and all the sample sizes tested, the proposed method under both
homoscedastic and heteroscedastic cases always achieves nominal coverage and substantially out-
performs the methods for comparison. In Settings (IV) and (V) with α = 1/2 and Beta-distributed
102


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 103 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 103.
X2, subbagging exhibits pronounced undercoverage, with empirical coverage ranging from 83% to
87% across sample sizes, well below the nominal 95% level. In these settings, subbagging’s compa-
rable or shorter confidence interval length is misleading, as the intervals fail to achieve the desired
coverage. In contrast, both proposed methods maintain valid coverage around 93%–96% through-
out. SSS consistently achieves nominal coverage but suffer from efficiency loss due to the sample
split.

B
Proposed Methodology in Multiple Time Point Case
In this section, firstly we shall provide the corresponding problems settings for the optimal dynamic
treatment regimes in the multiple time point case in Appendix B.1. Then the methodology as well
as the theoretical results are presented in Appendix B.2.
B.1
Optimal Dynamic Treatment Regimes
We now extend our proposed method to multiple time point case. Firstly, we give a brief review
of the optimal treatment regimes in multiple time point case which is similar to that of Shi et al.
(2020). For each subject, we assume the observation takes the following form:

Like the single time point case, here we also need similar assumptions to guarantee the validity
of our proposed method. In particular, Assumption 16 is exactly Assumption (C4.) in Shi et al.
(2020), which allows for polynomial unbounded density of τk.
Assumption 17 and 18 together
are weaker than Assumption (C5.) in Shi et al. (2020), which is similar to the single time point
case. Assumption 19 is just the common doubly robust rate requirement, which can be found at
Chernozhukov et al. (2018) and is weaker than the rate requirements in Theorem 3 of Shi et al.
(2020).

Assumption 20 (Stagewise rate requirement for tuning) There exists β ∈(0, α) such that, for
every k = 1, . . . , K and j = 1, 2,
EHk|bτk,Ij(Hk) −τk(Hk)|2 ≺p n−

Condition (C1-M) (stagewise near-parametric rate).
EHk|bτk,I(Hk) −τk(Hk)|2 = Op(log n/n) for every stage and fold.
Condition (C2-M) (stagewise stochastic boundedness). ∥τk∥∞< ∞and ∥bτk,I∥∞= Op(1)
uniformly over the finite set of stages and folds.
Condition (C3-M) (stagewise variability among splits). For every stage k and any two
disjoint relevant folds I, I′,
EHk|bτk,I(Hk) −bτk,I′(Hk)|2 ≍p EHk|bτk,I(Hk) −τk(Hk)|2.
The roles of these conditions are the stagewise counterparts of Conditions (C1)–(C3). Condi-
tion (C1-M) makes the truncation floor dominate both the stage-k squared error and its EAE,
without requiring Conditions (C2-M) or (C3-M). In the other branch, Condition (C2-M) bounds
the stagewise validation-fold summands and provides conditional concentration, while Condition
110


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 111 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 111.
(C3-M) links the population split discrepancy to the stage-k squared estimation error.
The same bias–variance interpretation applies at every stage. For two independently trained
stage-k fits on equal-sized folds, averaging over their training samples gives
E EHk|bτk,I(Hk) −bτk,I′(Hk)|2 = 2EHk[Var{bτk,I(Hk)}] ,
whereas the expected stage-k squared error is
E EHk|bτk,I(Hk) −τk(Hk)|2 = EHk[Var{bτk,I(Hk)}] + EHk[E{bτk,I(Hk)} −τk(Hk)]2 .
Thus Condition (C3-M) requires, at each stage, that squared bias not dominate variance and that
the realized quadratic quantities concentrate. The sufficient conditions in Lemmas A.2 and A.3
apply stage by stage after replacing (X, τ, bτI, EX) with (Hk, τk, bτk,I, EHk). In particular, the para-
metric lemma gives the stagewise moment comparison and Condition (C1-M), whereas the stagewise
versions of (U1)–(U4) give the stagewise moment comparison and Conditions (C2-M) and (C3-M).
Uniform constants and the fixed number of stages make these conclusions simultaneous by a finite
union bound.
Proposition B.1 Suppose Assumption 20 holds and either Condition (C1-M) holds or both Con-
ditions (C2-M) and (C3-M) hold. Then, for every k and j, the tuning selected by Algorithm 5
satisfies Assumptions 17 and 18.

here as what we did in the single time point case. For simplicity we will still focus on the case of
homoscedasticity in the beginning, i.e.,

We still use cubic spline to estimate the propensity scores π and conditional mean functions
µ in each stage, and the truncation for π in each stage is the same as in the single time point
case. In this simulation, the tuning hyperparameters, i.e., C0 for our proposed method and K0
for subbagging, take the same values as in the single time point case. The resampling times B for
all the methods are 1000, the sample size n = 600 and the observed results including Empirical
Coverage Probability (ECP) and Average Length of confidence intervals (AL) are aggregated over
500 Monte Carlo samples.
From Table 11, we see that the proposed method aims for the nominal coverage probability;
i.e. 95%, well in the regular case (H), and nonregular cases, Scenarios (F) and (G), which is in
118


===== ORIGINAL PDF PAGE 119 =====
FORMULA CHECK REQUIRED: original PDF page 119.
accordance with the asymptotic theory in Corollary B.1 and Corollary B.2. The results in Table
11 demonstrates that in all the scenarios whenever it is regular or not, the proposed method has
shortest confidence interval among all the competing methods. In particular, in the nonregular
cases, Scenarios (F) and (G), the efficiency gain of the proposed method over the current state-
of-the-art method, subbagging, is significant with about 5% reduction of average length from the
proposed method over that from subbaging.
C
Proofs of Theoretical Results in Multiple Time Point Case
In this section, we start with stating and proving a general technical lemma and then apply it to
justify the rest theoretical results in the multiple time point case.

D
Extensions to Heteroscedasticity in Multiple Time Point Case
In this section, we mainly discuss how to achieve the optimal efficiency in the case of heteroscedas-
ticity in multiple time point case. Compared with that in the single time point case, the situation
is a bit more complex and decision functions at each stage which optimize the efficiency need be
defined sequentially. In specific, with some calculation we find the following decision function is
optimal:
D(K)( ¯A(K−1), ¯X(K))
=I{τK( ¯A(K−1), ¯X(K)) > 0} + I{τK( ¯A(K−1), ¯X(K)) = 0}

−1
· Var(Y | ¯A(K−1), A(K) = 0, ¯X(K))πK( ¯A(K−1), A(K) = 1, ¯X(K))
(186)
and
D(k)( ¯A(k−1), ¯X(k))

We further provide additional simulations in the multiple time point case under heteroscedas-
ticity and α < 1 nonregularity. We consider three settings (VIII)–(X) that extend the two-stage
model in Section B.4 of the supplementary material. All three settings share the data generating
model
